**Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva**[^cite_note-3] (born **Luiz Inácio da Silva**; 27 October 1945), known [mononymously](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mononym) as **Lula**, is a Brazilian politician, trade unionist and former metalworker who has served as [president of Brazil](273638.md) since 2023 and from 2003 to 2011. He is a member of the [Workers' Party](224622.md).

Born in [Pernambuco](229525.md), Lula quit school after second grade to work, and did not learn to read until he was ten years old. As a teenager, he worked as a metalworker and became a [trade unionist](17626.md). Between 1978 and 1980, he led the [ABC workers' strikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978%E2%80%931980_ABC_Paulista_strikes) during Brazil's [military dictatorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_dictatorship_in_Brazil), and in 1980, he helped start the Workers' Party during [Brazil's redemocratization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redemocratization_in_Brazil). Lula was one of the leaders of the 1984 *[Diretas Já](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diretas_J%C3%A1)* movement, which demanded direct elections. In [1986](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1986_Brazilian_legislative_election), he was elected a federal deputy in the state of [São Paulo](229422.md). He ran for president in [1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_Brazilian_presidential_election), but lost in the second round. He also lost presidential elections in [1994](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Brazilian_general_election) and [1998](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Brazilian_general_election). He finally became president in [2002](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Brazilian_presidential_election), in a runoff. In [2006](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Brazilian_presidential_election), he was successfully re-elected in the second round.

Described as left-wing, his [first presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_presidency_of_Lula_da_Silva) coincided with South America's first [pink tide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pink_tide). During his first two consecutive terms in office, he continued fiscal policies and promoted social welfare programs such as [Bolsa Família](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsa_Fam%C3%ADlia) that eventually led to GDP growth, reduction in external debt and inflation, and helping millions of Brazilians escape poverty. He also played a role in foreign policy, both on a regional level and as part of global trade and environment negotiations.[^cite_note-4] During those terms, Lula was considered one of the most popular politicians in Brazil's history and left office with 80% approval rating.[^cite_note-auto6-5][^cite_note-6][^cite_note-7] His first term was also marked by notable corruption scandals, including the [Mensalão vote-buying scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mensal%C3%A3o_scandal). After the [2010 Brazilian general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Brazilian_general_election), he was succeeded by his former chief of staff, [Dilma Rousseff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dilma_Rousseff), and remained active in politics and gave lectures.

In July 2017, Lula was convicted on charges of [money laundering](19390.md) and corruption in the [Operation Car Wash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Car_Wash) context. He was [arrested in April 2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imprisonment_of_Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva) and went on to spend a total of 580 days in prison. Lula attempted to run in the [2018 Brazilian presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Brazilian_presidential_election), but was disqualified under Brazil's *[Ficha Limpa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ficha_Limpa)* law. He was convicted again in February 2019, and was released from prison the following November. His two convictions were nullified in 2021 by the [Supreme Federal Court](3359076.md), in a ruling which also found serious biases in the first case against him, also annulling all other pending cases. Once legally allowed to make another run for the presidency, Lula did so in the [2022 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Brazilian_general_election) and ultimately defeated the incumbent [Jair Bolsonaro](46603496.md) in a runoff. Sworn in on 1 January 2023 at the age of 77, he became the oldest Brazilian president at time of inauguration, as well as the first to have defeated an incumbent president and to be elected to a third term.

During his [second presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_presidency_of_Lula_da_Silva), Lula faced the [8 January attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/8_January_Bras%C3%ADlia_attacks) on federal institutions and advanced some institutional and economic reforms, including the approval of a landmark [tax reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_tax_reform) and a new fiscal framework. Although the period saw GDP growth, lower inflation and unemployment, and a decline in income inequality, persistent fiscal deficits contributed to rising public debt and relatively high interest rates. His administration has also emphasized environmental policy, renewed international engagement, and played a leading role in negotiations that enabled the preliminary approval of the [Mercosur–European Union trade agreement](61184322.md). On 2 August 2026, Lula formally launched his bid for reelection in the [October elections](72657051.md).[^cite_note-8]

## Early life



Lula and his sister Maria in 1949

Luiz Inácio da Silva was born on 27 October 1945[^cite_note-Britannica-9] (registered with a date of birth of 6 October 1945) in [Caetés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caet%C3%A9s,_Pernambuco) (then a district of [Garanhuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Garanhuns)), 250 km (160 mi) from [Recife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recife), capital of [Pernambuco](229525.md), a state in the [Northeast of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeast_of_Brazil). He was the seventh of eight children of Aristides Inácio da Silva and Eurídice Ferreira de Melo, farmers who had experienced famine in one of the poorest parts of the [agreste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agreste).[^cite_note-\:1-10] He was raised [Catholic](606848.md).[^cite_note-11] Lula's mother was of [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Brazilian) and partial [Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Brazilian) descent.[^cite_note-12] Two weeks after Lula's birth, his father moved to [Santos, São Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santos,_S%C3%A3o_Paulo), with – though Eurídice was not aware of it – her younger cousin Valdomira Ferreira de Góis.[^cite_note-Bearak270504-13]

In December 1952, when Lula was seven years old, his mother moved the family to [São Paulo](229422.md) to rejoin her husband. After a journey of 13 days in a *pau-de-arara* (open [truck bed](47877.md#Pickup_bed_styles)), they arrived in [Guarujá](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guaruj%C3%A1) and discovered that Aristides had formed a second family with Valdomira, with whom he had 10 more children.[^cite_note-14] Aristides's two families lived in the same house for some time, but they did not get along very well, and four years later, his mother moved with him and his siblings to a small room behind a bar in [São Paulo](390875.md). After that, Lula rarely saw his father, who died illiterate and an alcoholic in 1978.[^cite_note-15][^cite_note-story-16] In 1982, he added the nickname Lula to his legal name.[^cite_note-17][^cite_note-18]

## Personal life

Twice a [widower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Widower), Lula has been married three times, and has a daughter from a fourth relationship. In 1969, he married Maria de Lourdes Ribeiro.[^cite_note-cunning-19] She died of hepatitis in 1971 while pregnant with a child, who also died.[^cite_note-Hoje_em_dia-20]

In March 1974, Lula had a daughter, Lurian, with his then-girlfriend, Miriam Cordeiro.[^cite_note-cunning-19] The two never married. Lula only began participating in his daughter's life when she was already a young adult.[^cite_note-21]

Two months later, in May 1974, Lula married [Marisa Letícia Rocco Casa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marisa_Let%C3%ADcia_Lula_da_Silva), a 24-year-old widow whom he had met the prior year. He had three sons with her, and adopted her son from her first marriage.[^cite_note-cunning-19] The two remained married for 43 years, until her death on 2 February 2017, after a stroke.[^cite_note-\:4-22]

Later that same year, he met and started a relationship with [Rosângela da Silva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ros%C3%A2ngela_Lula_da_Silva), known as Janja. The relationship only became public in 2019 while he was serving time in jail in [Curitiba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curitiba), Paraná, on corruption charges.[^cite_note-23] Lula and Janja married on 18 May 2022.[^cite_note-\:2-24]

Lula is [Catholic](606848.md) and supporter of the football club [SC Corinthians Paulista](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SC_Corinthians_Paulista).[^cite_note-25][^cite_note-26]

## Education and work



Lula had his left [little finger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_finger) [amputated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amputated) after a work-related accident when he was a metalworker in 1964.

Lula had little formal education. He did not learn to read until he was ten years old.[^cite_note-Fourth-time-27] He quit school after the second grade to work. His first job at age eight was as a street vendor.[^cite_note-\:3-28] When he was 12, he also worked as a shoeshiner. In 1960, when he was 14, he got his first formal job, in a warehouse.[^cite_note-Biography-29]

In 1961, he started working as an apprentice of a press operator in a metallurgical company that produced screws, while studying in a vocational course. There, Lula had his first contact with strike movements.[^cite_note-30] After the movement failed in its negotiations, Lula left the company for another metallurgical company. From 1966 to 1980, he worked at [Villares Metals S.A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Villares_Metals_S.A?action=edit\&redlink=1), a new metalworking firm.[^cite_note-cunning-19]

There, in 1964, at the age of 19, he lost his left pinky finger in a machinery accident, while working as a press operator in the factory.[^cite_note-Fourth-time-27] After the accident, he had to run to several hospitals before he received medical attention. This experience increased his interest in participating in the Workers' Union. Around that time, he became involved in union activities and held several union posts.[^cite_note-Biography-29][^cite_note-LULAC1-31]

## Union career



Lula's [DOPS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Political_and_Social_Order) mugshot, 1980

Inspired by his brother Frei Chico, a member of the [Brazilian Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Communist_Party_\(1922\)?action=edit\&redlink=1), Lula joined the [labour movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_movement) when he worked at Villares Metals, rising through the ranks.[^cite_note-32] He was elected in 1975, and re-elected in 1978, as president of the [ABC Metalworkers' Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABC_Metalworkers'_Union) of [São Bernardo do Campo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A3o_Bernardo_do_Campo) and [Diadema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diadema,_Brazil). Both cities are located in the [ABCD Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABCD_Region), home to most of Brazil's automobile manufacturing facilities, including [Ford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ford_do_Brasil), [Volkswagen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volkswagen_do_Brasil), [Toyota](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toyota), and [Mercedes-Benz](18564164.md).

In the late 1970s, when Brazil was under military rule, Lula helped organize union activities, including major [strikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978-1980_ABC_Paulista_strikes). [Labour courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labour_court) found the strikes illegal, and in 1980, Lula was jailed for a month. Due to this, and like other people imprisoned for political activities under the military government, Lula was awarded a lifetime pension after the fall of the military regime.[^cite_note-story-16]

## Political career

On 10 February 1980, a group of academics and union leaders, including Lula, founded the *[Partido dos Trabalhadores](224622.md)* (PT) or Workers' Party, a left-wing party with [progressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressivism) ideas.[^cite_note-33][^cite_note-34][^cite_note-35][^cite_note-36] In 1983, he helped found the [Central Única dos Trabalhadores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_%C3%9Anica_dos_Trabalhadores) (CUT) trade union association.[^cite_note-37]



Lula speaking at the plenary of the [Chamber of Deputies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamber_of_Deputies_of_Brazil), 1989

### Elections

Lula first ran for office in 1982 for the state government of [São Paulo](229422.md), but lost with 11% of the vote. Cuban president [Fidel Castro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Castro) urged him to continue on as a politician, during a trip by Lula to Cuba.[^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39] In the 1986 election, Lula won a seat in the [National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Congress_of_Brazil) with the most votes nationwide.[^cite_note-40]

In 1989, Lula ran for president as the PT candidate. Lula advocated immediate land reform and that Brazil [default](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Default_\(finance\)) on its [external debt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/External_debt). A minor candidate, [Fernando Collor de Mello](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Collor_de_Mello), quickly amassed support with a more business-friendly agenda and by taking emphatic anti-corruption positions. He beat Lula in the second round of the [1989 elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1989_Brazilian_presidential_election). Lula decided not to run for re-election as a Congressman in 1990.[^cite_note-41]

Lula ran again for president, and lost again, in the next two Brazilian elections. Former [PSDB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PSDB) Minister of Finance [Fernando Henrique Cardoso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Henrique_Cardoso) defeated Lula who received only 27% of the vote in the presidential elections in [1994](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1994_Brazilian_general_election), and again, by a somewhat smaller margin, as Lula garnered only 32% of the vote in [1998](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_Brazilian_general_election).

An article in *[The Washington Post](102226.md)* said that before 2002, Lula had been a "strident [union organizer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_organizer) known for his bushy beard and [Che Guevara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Che_Guevara) T-shirts".[^cite_note-WPost-42] In the 2002 campaign, Lula abandoned both his informal clothing style and his platform plank that Brazil should not pay its foreign debt unless it links the payment to a prior thorough audit. This last point had worried economists, businessmen, and banks, who feared that even a partial Brazilian default would have a massive ripple effect through the world economy. Lula in the [2002 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Brazilian_general_election), defeated PSDB candidate [José Serra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Serra) in a runoff, to become the country's first [leftist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leftist) president following the fall of the [military dictatorship in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_dictatorship_in_Brazil).[^cite_note-43] In the [2006 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2006_Brazilian_general_election), Lula won a [run-off](25932.md) over the PSDB's [Geraldo Alckmin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geraldo_Alckmin).[^cite_note-2006-election-44]

In September 2018, Brazil's top electoral court banned Lula from running for president in [2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Brazilian_general_election) due to his corruption conviction, in accordance with Brazil's *[Lei da Ficha Limpa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lei_da_Ficha_Limpa)* law.[^cite_note-45] Instead, [Fernando Haddad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Haddad) ran for president on the Workers Party ticket, and was defeated by [Jair Bolsonaro](46603496.md).[^cite_note-46]

## First presidency (2003–2011)

Main article: [First presidency of Lula da Silva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_presidency_of_Lula_da_Silva)





Lula at the beginning of his first (left) and second (right) terms

Lula, described as left-wing, served two terms as president from 2003 through 2010.[^cite_note-\:1-10][^cite_note-\:2-24] During his farewell speech, he said he felt a burden to prove that he could handle the presidency despite his humble beginnings. "If I failed, it would be the workers' class which would be failing; it would be this country's poor who would be proving they did not have what it takes to rule".[^cite_note-47][^cite_note-galeria-48]

### Political orientation

Very few of the reforms that Lula proposed were actually implemented during Lula's terms of office. Some wings of the Worker's Party disagreed with the increasing moderation in focus since the late eighties, and left the party to form other parties, such as during Lula's presidency, the [Socialism and Liberty Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism_and_Liberty_Party). Alliances with old, traditional oligarch politicians, like former presidents [José Sarney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Sarney) and Fernando Collor, have been a cause of disappointment for some.[^cite_note-49]

### Education

A number of educational initiatives were launched during Lula's first presidency. A free school meals programme was extended to 37 million pupils while a programme was launched which aimed to provide "whole or partial remission of student fees for low-income students".[^cite_note-story-16] In 2006, primary education was extended from 8 to 9 years. A Fund for the Maintenance and Development of Basic Education was set up to improve the quality of education. The PED (an education development plan) conditioned the disbursement of public funds to state schools on the schools' performance.[^cite_note-50]

Still in 2006, many Brazilians and commentators felt that Lula had not done enough to improve the quality of public education.[^cite_note-51][^cite_note-52][^cite_note-53] And in 2010, while education was compulsory for all children in Brazil aged 7 to 14, in practice that requirement was only loosely enforced; 90% of children in rural areas attended school for less than four years, and only 25% of children living in favelas attended school.[^cite_note-54]

### Social programs

Lula's top social programme sought to eradicate hunger. It was financed by an increase in tax revenues, coupled with a decrease in government expenditures on both wages and on benefits paid to public employees, as well as a decrease in government expenditures on infrastructure.[^cite_note-55] The programme followed the lead of a project that had already been put into practice in 1995 by the Fernando Henrique Cardoso administration, which was named *[Bolsa Escola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_policy_in_Brazil#Bolsa_Escola)* (School Stipend).[^cite_note-56] It expanded that initiative with the new *[Fome Zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fome_Zero)* ("Zero Hunger") programme, which was part of the *[Bolsa Família](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsa_Fam%C3%ADlia)* (Family Allowance) plan.[^cite_note-57][^cite_note-58]

Five months after Lula took office, however, the budget for *Fome Zero* was cut down a third from its original amount, and one year later, about \$800 million was budgeted toward the programme, but only \$130 million of that was actually disbursed.[^cite_note-59] Lula's programme was accused of having become more bark than bite, inasmuch as of May 2005, two years after the effort began, the programme had fallen far short of expectations.[^cite_note-60]



Lula speech in [Diadema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diadema,_S%C3%A3o_Paulo) launching [subsidized housing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsidized_housing) and *[Bolsa Família](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolsa_Fam%C3%ADlia)* credits, 2005

During Lula's first term, [child malnutrition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Undernutrition_in_children) in Brazil decreased by 46%. In May 2010, the UN [World Food Programme](33582.md) (WFP) awarded Lula the title of "World Champion in the Fight against Hunger".[^cite_note-61] A number of other social projects were introduced during Lula's first presidency.[^cite_note-62]

Lula launched a housing aid programme that was larger in scope to the policies developed until then. More than 15 billion euros were invested in water purification and the urbanization of [favelas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favela), and more than 40 billion in housing. The government proposed to relocate the poor populations that occupied the "risk zones", prone to floods or landslides; at the end of the day, however, at least 212 people died and at least 15,000 people were made homeless by the [April 2010 Rio de Janeiro floods and mudslides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/April_2010_Rio_de_Janeiro_floods_and_mudslides) alone. It proposed to then extend the electricity network, to relocate the streets and to improve the precarious housing. The government undertook to democratize access to real estate credit.[^cite_note-63]

### Economy

Lula's first two terms coincided with a strong [boom in commodities prices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000s_commodities_boom).[^cite_note-auto36-64][^cite_note-auto37-65][^cite_note-auto35-66] This fueled an economic boom in Brazil, which in turn allowed Lula to spend heavily on social programmes and pay off a \$15 billion IMF loan a year early.[^cite_note-auto36-64][^cite_note-auto37-65][^cite_note-auto35-66]

In the run-up to the 2002 elections, the fear of Lula taking drastic measures, and comparisons of him with [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez) of [Venezuela](32374.md), increased internal market speculation. This led to a drop in the value of the [Brazilian real](34201323.md), and a downgrade of Brazil's [credit rating](1194146.md).[^cite_note-Downgrade-67] Lula chose [Henrique Meirelles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henrique_Meirelles) of the [Brazilian Social Democracy Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Social_Democracy_Party) (PSDB), a market-oriented economist, as head of the [Brazilian Central Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Central_Bank). As a former CEO of [BankBoston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BankBoston) he was well known to the market.[^cite_note-MeirBio-68]

Lula and his cabinet followed, to an extent, the lead of the previous government in economics.[^cite_note-69] It renewed all agreements with the [International Monetary Fund](15251.md) (IMF), which were signed by the time Argentina defaulted on its own deals in 2001. His government achieved a satisfactory primary [budget surplus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budget_surplus) in the first two years, as required by the IMF agreement, exceeding the target for the third year. In late 2005, the government paid off its debt to the IMF in full, two years ahead of schedule.[^cite_note-IMF-70] The Brazilian economy was generally not affected by the 2005 [Mensalão scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mensal%C3%A3o_scandal), which related to vote buying in the Brazilian Congress.[^cite_note-71]



Chief of staff [José Dirceu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Dirceu), Lula, and finance minister [Antonio Palocci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Palocci), 2003

In June 2005, economist and attorney [José Dirceu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Dirceu), Lula's chief-of-staff since 2003, resigned after he was caught up in a massive corruption scheme in the legislature, the Mensalão corruption scandal.[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73][^cite_note-auto27-74][^cite_note-75] In March 2006, Lula's finance minister [Antonio Palocci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antonio_Palocci), who had continued the anti-inflation and pro-market policies of the previous centrist government, resigned due to his involvement in a corruption and [abuse of power](4308572.md) scandal.[^cite_note-76][^cite_note-77][^cite_note-78] Lula then appointed [Guido Mantega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guido_Mantega), a PT economist, as finance minister.

Not long after the start of his second term, in 2007 Lula's government announced the [Growth Acceleration Programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Growth_Acceleration_Program) (*_Programa de Aceleração de Crescimento_*, PAC), an investment programme which sought to solve many of the problems that prevented the Brazilian economy from expanding more rapidly. The measures included investment in the creation and repair of roads and railways, simplification and reduction of taxation, and modernization of the country's energy production to avoid further shortages. The money pledged to be spent on this programme was to be around [R\$](34201323.md) 500 billion (US \$260 billion) over four years. However, by 2010 many projects remained mired in bureaucracy, and only 11% of the projects outlined in the plan had been completed, while just over half had not even been launched.[^cite_note-auto26-79]

Prior to taking office, Lula had been a critic of [privatization](24661.md).[^cite_note-80] His administration created [public-private partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public-private_partnership) concessions for seven federal roadways.[^cite_note-81] After decades with the largest [foreign debt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_debt) among [emerging economies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emerging_economies), Brazil became a net creditor for the first time in January 2008.[^cite_note-82] By mid-2008, both [Fitch Ratings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fitch_Ratings) and [Standard & Poor's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_&_Poor's) had elevated the classification of Brazilian debt from speculative to investment grade. Banks made record profits under Lula's government.[^cite_note-83]



Lula and his wife [Marisa Letícia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marisa_Let%C3%ADcia_Lula_da_Silva) review troops during the 2007 [Independence Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independence_Day_\(Brazil\)) military parade

The [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis) might have been a tsunami in the US and Europe, Lula declared, but in Brazil it would be no more than a little 'ripple' ("*uma marolinha*"). The phrase was seized on by the Brazilian press as proof of Lula's reckless economic ignorance and irresponsibility.[^cite_note-84] In 2008, Brazil enjoyed economic good health to mitigate the 2008 financial crisis with a large [economic stimulus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_stimulus) lasting, at least, until 2014.[^cite_note-85] According to *[The Washington Post](102226.md)*: "Under Lula, Brazil became the world's eighth-largest economy, \[and] more than 20 million people rose out of acute poverty ..."[^cite_note-WPost-42]

At the same time, in 2010 *The Wall Street Journal* noted that: "\[Brazil's] [public sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_sector) is bloated and riddled with corruption. Crime is rampant. Its infrastructure is badly in need of repair and expansion. The business environment is restrictive, with a labour code ripped from the pages of [Benito Mussolini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benito_Mussolini)'s economic playbook. Brazil also risks patting itself on the back so much that it fails to see the colossal work that remains to be done."[^cite_note-auto26-79]

### Environment



Brazil's deforestation rate declined during Lula's time in office, and reversed under Bolsonaro

[^cite_note-Axios_20221031-86]

[^cite_note-TerraBrasilis_1988-87]

Initially, Lula's administration pushed for progressive policies that significantly curbed deforestation in the Amazon. Despite this, he did not support legislation that would have required the country to phase out its fossil fuels.

In May 2008, environmental minister [Marina Silva](17484359.md) resigned, blamed "stagnation" in the government, after she lost disagreements with Lula when she opposed approval of new [hydroelectric dams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroelectric_dam) in the Amazon and criticized Lula's [biofuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biofuels) programme.[^cite_note-88] Dr. Daniel Nepstad of the [Woods Hole Research Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woods_Hole_Research_Center) said the growing demand for biofuels may ultimately result in more Amazon deforestation.[^cite_note-89] In particular, environmentalists warn that while biofuels reduce emissions of [greenhouse gases](21350772.md), they may well also trigger a significant expansion of the biofuel crops; that, in turn, may push agriculture deeper into forests, destroying habitat and [biodiversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity).[^cite_note-90]

The creation of conservation areas and indigenous reserves led to a decrease of around 75% in deforestation starting in 2004.[^cite_note-91] In Lula's first year in office, in 2003–04, 25,000 [square kilometers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Square_kilometer) of Brazilian forest were destroyed, the second-worst devastation since 1977.[^cite_note-auto32-92] In late 2006, the Instituto Soicioambiental environmental group said that deforestation in Lula's first four years had been worse than in any four-year period since 1988.[^cite_note-93][^cite_note-auto32-92][^cite_note-94] By 2009, Brazil's Amazon destruction—though lower—was still about 7,000 square kilometers a year, larger than the US state of [Delaware](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delaware).[^cite_note-auto29-95] Critics said, however, that Brazil's lowest rate of deforestation in 2009 was a function of the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis).[^cite_note-auto30-96] Paulo Adario of [Greenpeace](12233.md) said that it was a function not of efforts to protect the climate, but of the fact that the "demand for beef, soya and wood ha\[d] dramatically fallen".[^cite_note-auto30-96]

In 2009, Lula gave a speech in which he said that "gringos" should pay Amazon nations to prevent deforestation.[^cite_note-auto29-95]

In February 2010, Lula's government approved the construction of a controversial hydroelectric mega [Belo Monte Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belo_Monte_Dam) in the middle of the Amazon rain forest in the Brazilian state of [Pará](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Par%C3%A1).[^cite_note-auto30-96] It was to be the third-largest hydroelectric dam in the world.[^cite_note-auto30-96] Environmental activists protested the building of the dam.[^cite_note-auto30-96] It was expected to cause a significant decline in the [water table](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_table), resulting in significant losses of aquatic and terrestrial [fauna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fauna), and adversely impact [aquatic mammals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquatic_mammal).[^cite_note-auto33-97] Approximately 20,000-40,000 indigenous people were to be resettled with little or no compensation, and 516 square kilometers (199 square miles) of rain forest were to be flooded for the dam's construction.[^cite_note-auto30-96][^cite_note-auto33-97]

### Foreign policy

Main article: [Foreign relations of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Brazil)



U.S. President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) greets Lula in the [Oval Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oval_Office), 2009



Lula with Turkish Prime Minister [Recep Tayyip Erdoğan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan) negotiated a failed 2010 [Iran nuclear fuel swap deal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran's_nuclear_fuel_swap)



Lula with leaders from Russia, China, and India at the [2nd BRIC summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2nd_BRIC_summit)

In 1979, Lula was asked in an interview which historical figures he admired most. He answered: [Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gandhi), [Che Guevara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Che_Guevara), and [Mao Zedong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mao_Zedong).[^cite_note-auto28-98] Upon being asked to give additional examples, he added [Fidel Castro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Castro), [Ruhollah Khomeini](26232.md) and [Adolf Hitler](2731583.md) and saying about the latter: "I admire in a man the fire to want to do something, and then his going out to try to do it."[^cite_note-Bearak270504-13][^cite_note-auto28-98]

Leading a large agricultural state, Lula generally opposed and criticized farm subsidies, and this position has been seen as one of the reasons for the walkout of developing nations and subsequent collapse of the Cancún [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization) talks in 2003 over [G8](59755.md) agricultural subsidies.[^cite_note-time-99] Brazil played a role in negotiations regarding internal conflicts in Venezuela and [Colombia](5222.md), and made efforts to strengthen [Mercosur](25869317.md).[^cite_note-ft-100] During the Lula administration, Brazilian foreign trade increased dramatically, changing from deficits to several surpluses after 2003. In 2004, the surplus was US\$29 billion, due to a substantial increase in global demand for commodities. Brazil also provided UN peacekeeping troops and led a peacekeeping mission in [Haiti](13373.md).[^cite_note-101]

According to *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* of 2 March 2006, Lula had a pragmatic foreign policy, seeing himself as a negotiator, not an ideologue, a leader adept at reconciling opposites. As a result, he befriended both Venezuelan president [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez) and U.S. President [George W. Bush](3414021.md).[^cite_note-102] Former [Finance Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finance_Minister), and current advisor, Delfim Netto, said: "Lula is the ultimate pragmatist".[^cite_note-103]

He travelled to more than 80 countries during his presidency.[^cite_note-al_jaz-104] A goal of Lula's foreign policy was for the country to gain a seat as a permanent member of the United Nations Security Council. In this he was unsuccessful.[^cite_note-al_jaz-104]

#### China

From 2003 to 2010, Lula embraced China as central to reforming what he considered an unjust global order.[^cite_note-auto11-105] He intertwined the Chinese and Brazilian economies.[^cite_note-106][^cite_note-107][^cite_note-auto11-105] Lula stated Brazil's commitment to the One China principle that is the position held by the [People's Republic of China](5405.md) and the ruling [Chinese Communist Party](7175.md), saying that the government of the People's Republic of China was the sole legal government representing the whole of China, including [Taiwan](25734.md)—as part of China.[^cite_note-108]

#### Cuba



Lula and Cuban leader [Fidel Castro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Castro), 2003

Lula and Cuban president [Fidel Castro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fidel_Castro) were longtime friends.[^cite_note-109][^cite_note-110] Under Lula, Brazil provided money and corporate support to Cuba.[^cite_note-111][^cite_note-auto3-112] The state-controlled Brazilian oil company [Petrobras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrobras) studied the possibility of drilling for oil off of Cuba, while the [Odebrecht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odebrecht) construction firm headed a revamp of the Cuban port of [Mariel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariel,_Cuba) into the island's main commercial port.[^cite_note-auto3-112][^cite_note-auto2-113] Brazil's state-run [Brazilian Development Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Development_Bank) gave \$300 million to Odebrecht to build new roads, rail lines, wharves, and warehouses at Mariel.[^cite_note-auto3-112] Brazil also offered Cuba up to \$1 billion in [credit lines](4611307.md) to pay for Brazilian goods and services.[^cite_note-auto2-113]

#### Iran



Lula with [Supreme Leader of Iran](36814344.md) [Ali Khamenei](385653.md), 2010

The conviction by an Iranian court of Iranian [Sakineh Mohammadi Ashtiani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sakineh_Mohammadi_Ashtiani) for the crime of adultery, with a sentence in 2006 of execution by stoning, led to calls for Lula to intercede on her behalf. In July 2010, Lula said "I need to respect the laws of a \[foreign] country. If my friendship with the [president of Iran](233913.md) and the respect that I have for him is worth something, if this woman has become a nuisance, we will receive her in Brazil". The Iranian government declined his offer.[^cite_note-washingtonpost1-114][^cite_note-115] [Mina Ahadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mina_Ahadi), an Iranian Communist politician, welcomed Lula's offer, but reiterated a call for an end to stoning altogether and requested a cessation of recognition and support for the Iranian government.[^cite_note-116][^cite_note-117][^cite_note-118][^cite_note-119] [Jackson Diehl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jackson_Diehl), deputy [editorial page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Editorial_page) editor of *[The Washington Post](102226.md)*, called Lula the "best friend of tyrants in the democratic world," and criticised his actions.[^cite_note-washingtonpost1-114] [Shirin Ebadi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shirin_Ebadi), Iranian human rights activist and Nobel Peace Prize laureate called Lula's comments a "powerful message to the Islamic Republic".[^cite_note-120]

In 2009, Lula warmly hosted Iranian president [Ahmadinejad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ahmadinejad), who made a controversial visit to Brazil.[^cite_note-121][^cite_note-122] Some demonstrators expressed displeasure over Ahmadinejad's positions on human rights and his denial of [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust).[^cite_note-123]

In May 2010, Lula and Turkey's prime minister [Recep Tayyip Erdoğan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recep_Tayyip_Erdo%C4%9Fan) negotiated a preliminary fuel swap agreement with the Iranian government on [uranium enrichment](37555.md), that ultimately failed.[^cite_note-auto19-124] The preliminary agreement that they presented to the United Nations was at odds with what the [International Atomic Energy Agency](14984.md) and other countries viewed as necessary actions to stop Iran from obtaining [weapons grade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weapons_grade) materials.[^cite_note-auto19-124] Within hours of signing the agreement, Iran did an about-face and announced that it would continue to enrich some uranium.[^cite_note-auto20-125] U.S. Secretary of State [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton) said Brazil was being "used" by Tehran.[^cite_note-auto20-125] The [UN Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Security_Council) ultimately rejected it when permanent member country representatives argued that "the swap proposal negotiated by Brazil and Turkey would leave Iran with enough material to make a nuclear weapon," and that "Iran intends to continue a new programme of enriching uranium to a higher level."[^cite_note-auto19-124][^cite_note-al_jaz-104] [Pulitzer Prize](24230.md) winning journalist [Thomas Friedman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Friedman) wrote: "Is there anything uglier than watching democrats sell out other democrats to a Holocaust-denying, vote-stealing Iranian thug just to tweak the U.S. and show that they, too, can play at the big power table?"[^cite_note-auto21-126] [Moisés Naím](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mois%C3%A9s_Na%C3%ADm), editor in chief of *[Foreign Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_Policy)* magazine and former Minister of Trade in Venezuela, said "Lula is a political giant, but morally he has been a deep disappointment."[^cite_note-auto21-126] In 2010, in addition, Brazilians largely disagreed with Lula as to how to handle Iran and Iran's nuclear weapons programme.[^cite_note-auto10-127] While Lula opposed additional international economic sanctions against Iran, of the 85% of Brazilians who opposed Iran acquiring nuclear weapons, two-thirds approved of tighter international sanctions on Iran to try to prevent it from developing nuclear weapons.[^cite_note-auto10-127]

#### Iraq



Lula with U.S. President [George W. Bush](3414021.md) in November 2008

In 2003, Lula condemned the U.S.-led [invasion of Iraq](201936.md), saying that the United States had no right "to decide unilaterally what is good and what is bad for the world".[^cite_note-128] He said that "the behaviour of the United States in relation to Iraq has weakened the United Nations".[^cite_note-129]

#### Libya

Brazil, as a non-permanent member of the [UN Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Security_Council), abstained from the vote authorising "all necessary measures" against Libya's [Muammar Gaddafi](53029.md).[^cite_note-auto38-130] It opposed the [bombing in Libya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_military_intervention_in_Libya) to implement [United Nations Security Council Resolution 1973](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_1973).[^cite_note-auto38-130] Lula said: "These invasions only happen because the United Nations is weak."[^cite_note-auto38-130]

#### Venezuela

Lula was close with [Venezuela](32374.md)'s president [Hugo Chávez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_Ch%C3%A1vez), a close ally of Communist Cuba and an antagonist of the United States.[^cite_note-auto8-131][^cite_note-auto9-132] In November 2007, Lula defended Chávez as the democratic choice of his people.[^cite_note-auto9-132] He said: "There is no risk with Chávez."[^cite_note-auto9-132] Expressing his admiration for Chávez, he said "Only thanks to Chávez's leadership, the people \[of Venezuela] have had extraordinary achievements," and that in 2008 that Chávez was "the best president the country has had in 100 years."[^cite_note-133] However, in 2010 Brazilians largely had a different view than Lula, as only 13% had at least some confidence in Chávez, while 70% had little or no confidence in him.[^cite_note-auto10-127]

### Freedom of the press

After Lula was infuriated by a 9 May 2004, *New York Times* article that claimed he had a drinking problem, Brazil ordered the *New York Times* reporter, [Larry Rohter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larry_Rohter), to leave the country and revoked his visa because he had written a story "offensive to the honour of the president."[^cite_note-134][^cite_note-auto23-135] Lula said: "Certainly its author ... must be more worried than I am ... it deserves action."[^cite_note-auto23-135] Brazil's presidential palace threatened to take legal action against the *New York Times*, which stood by the story and said that the expulsion raised serious questions about [freedom of expression](21401843.md) and [freedom of the press](105323.md) in Brazil.[^cite_note-auto23-135] No journalist had been expelled from Brazil since its military dictatorship ended in the mid-1980s.[^cite_note-auto23-135] Brazilian opposition senator [Tasso Jereissati](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tasso_Jereissati) said: "This is ridiculous. It's more like the immature act of a dictator of a third-rate republic..."[^cite_note-auto23-135] Brazil's second largest union, [Força Sindical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/For%C3%A7a_Sindical), issued a statement expressing concern that: "it is a reaction typical of authoritarian governments that don't like contrary voices."[^cite_note-auto23-135] Despite criticism, on 13 May 2004, Lula said "he would not consider revoking the action."[^cite_note-auto23-135] The government subsequently changed its position, and allowed the reporter to remain.[^cite_note-auto22-136]

Three months later, Lula introduced legislation to create a Brazil National Journalists' Council that would have the power to "orient, discipline and monitor" journalists and their work.[^cite_note-auto22-136][^cite_note-auto24-137] Critics called the draft law the worst affront to press freedom since censorship under the military dictatorship.[^cite_note-auto22-136][^cite_note-auto24-137] The government also proposed the establishment of a National Cinema and Audiovisual Agency that would have the power to conduct prior reviews of programming and to veto certain programmes if they believed that they did not to meet standards of "editorial responsibility."[^cite_note-auto22-136]

## Corruption scandals and controversy

See also: [List of scandals in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_scandals_in_Brazil)

Lula's administration was plagued by numerous corruption scandals, notably the [Mensalão \$50 million vote-buying scandal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mensal%C3%A3o_scandal) and [Escândalo dos sanguessugas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esc%C3%A2ndalo_dos_sanguessugas?action=edit\&redlink=1) during Lula's first term.[^cite_note-138][^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140]

### Mensalão vote-buying scandal

In the 2005 Mensalão \$50 million vote-buying scandal, Brazilian attorney general Álvaro Augusto Ribeiro Costa presented charges against 40 politicians and officials involved in the Mensalão affair, including several charges against Lula himself. Lula said that he knew nothing about the scandals.[^cite_note-naosabia-141]

Among those convicted were [Jose Dirceu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jose_Dirceu) (who was Lula's chief of staff and right-hand man from 2003 to 2005; he was sentenced to over 10 years in jail), and both the former head of Lula's Workers Party, [Jose Genoino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jose_Genoino), and its treasurer, [Delubio Soares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delubio_Soares).[^cite_note-auto27-74][^cite_note-142] Dirceu and officials [Luiz Gushiken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luiz_Gushiken) and [Humberto Costa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humberto_Costa) said that Lula was not involved. [Roberto Jefferson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roberto_Jefferson) said that if Lula didn't "commit a crime by action, he committed it by omission."[^cite_note-143] But one of Lula's own party members, [Arlindo Chinaglia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arlindo_Chinaglia), alleged that Lula had been warned about the matter, and businessman [Marcos Valério](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcos_Val%C3%A9rio), who was sentenced to more than 40 years in prison for his involvement in the scandal, alleged in testimony after he was convicted that Lula had authorized loans for the scheme and used some of the money to pay for his personal expenses.[^cite_note-144][^cite_note-145]

### Politicking

His administration was criticized for relying on local, right-of-centre political barons, like [José Sarney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Sarney), [Jader Barbalho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jader_Barbalho), [Renan Calheiros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renan_Calheiros) and Fernando Collor to ensure a majority in [Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Brazil). Another frequent reproach was his ambiguous treatment of the left wing of the PT. Analysts felt that he would occasionally give in to left-wing calls for tighter government control on media and increased state intervention: in 2004, he pushed for the creation of a "Federal Council of Journalists" (CFJ) and a "National Cinema Agency" (Ancinav), the latter designed to overhaul funding for [electronic communications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_media). Both proposals ultimately failed amid concerns over the effect of state control on free speech.[^cite_note-146][^cite_note-147]

### Great Recession caused by white people with blue eyes

Before a [G-20 summit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G-20_summit) in London in March 2009, Lula caused an uproar by declaring that the [economic crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession) was caused by "the irrational behavior of white people with blue eyes, who before seemed to know everything, and now have shown they don't know anything".[^cite_note-148] He added: "I don't know any black or indigenous bankers."[^cite_note-auto5-149] He repeated the accusation the following month.[^cite_note-auto5-149]

### Terrorist Cesare Battisti

When Italian far-left terrorist [Cesare Battisti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cesare_Battisti_\(1954\)) of the [Armed Proletarians for Communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_Proletarians_for_Communism), wanted for four murders, was arrested in Rio de Janeiro in March 2007 by Brazilian and French police officers, Brazilian Minister of Justice [Tarso Genro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarso_Genro) granted him status as a political refugee. It was a controversial decision, which divided Italy and the Brazilian and international press. In February 2009, the [European Parliament](9581.md) adopted a resolution in support of Italy, and held a minute's silence in memory of Battisti's victims. In November 2009, the Brazilian Supreme Court declared the grant of refugee status illegal and allowed Battisti's extradition, but also stated that the Brazilian constitution gave the president the personal power to deny the extradition if he chose to, effectively putting the final decision in the hands of Lula.[^cite_note-150]

Lula barred Battisti's extradition.[^cite_note-151] On 31 December 2010, Lula's last day in office, his decision not to allow Battisti's extradition was officially announced. Battisti was released on 9 June 2011 from prison, after the Brazilian Constitutional Court denied Italy's request to extradite him. Italy planned to appeal to the [International Court of Justice](14918.md) in the Hague, saying Brazil had breached an extradition treaty.[^cite_note-152] President [Michel Temer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Temer) revoked his status as a permanent resident in December, 2018 and an arrest warrant was issued; Battisti then entered Bolivia illegally, and was arrested and extradited from Bolivia in January 2019.[^cite_note-153]

### Operation Zelotes

In 2015, Lula, along with his former chief of staff [Gilberto Carvalho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilberto_Carvalho) and five others, was indicted in a corruption probe as part of [Operation Zelotes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Zelotes?action=edit\&redlink=1), regarding payment of R\$6 million in bribes (US \$1.5 million). Prosecutors alleged they helped pass Provisional Measure 471 (which was later converted into Law 12,218/2010) in 2009 in order to benefit the automotive companies [Grupo Caoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grupo_Caoa) and MMC.[^cite_note-154] On 21 June 2021, Judge Frederico Botelho de Barros Viana of the 10th Federal Court of Brasilia acquitted all the accused, saying that the prosecution did not convincingly demonstrate that the defendants were involved in a criminal conspiracy.[^cite_note-155]

### Operation Car Wash: corruption investigation and prosecution



Protests related to 2016 Lula's testimony



Demonstrators gather in front of the [Palácio do Planalto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pal%C3%A1cio_do_Planalto), the presidential palace, to protest against Lula's appointment as [Chief of Staff of the Presidency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_of_Staff_of_Brazil), 16 March 2016



Lula is sworn in as Chief of Staff by President [Dilma Rousseff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dilma_Rousseff) on 17 March 2016.

In 2014, Brazil began *Operação Lava Jato* (English: [Operation Car Wash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Car_Wash)), resulting in multiple arrests and convictions, including nine suits against Lula.

In April 2015, the [Public Ministry](13013461.md) of Brazil opened an investigation into allegations of [influence peddling](630570.md) by Lula, which alleged that between 2011 and 2014 he had lobbied for government contracts in foreign countries for the [Odebrecht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Odebrecht) company and had also persuaded the Brazilian Development Bank to finance the projects in [Ghana](12067.md), [Angola](701.md), [Cuba](5042481.md), and the [Dominican Republic](8060.md).[^cite_note-156] In June 2015, [Marcelo Odebrecht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcelo_Odebrecht), president of Odebrecht, was arrested on charges that he had paid politicians \$230 million in bribes.[^cite_note-mo-157] Three other company executives were also arrested, as well as the chief executive of [Andrade Gutierrez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrade_Gutierrez), another construction conglomerate.[^cite_note-158]

On 4 March 2016, as part of "Operation Car Wash", Brazilian authorities raided Lula's home.[^cite_note-Romero-159][^cite_note-160] After the raid, the police detained Lula for questioning.[^cite_note-euronews-161][^cite_note-Machado-162] A police statement alleged that Lula had collaborated in illegal bribes from the oil company [Petrobras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrobras) to benefit his political party and presidential campaign.[^cite_note-euronews-161] Prosecutor Carlos Fernando said, "The favours to Lula from big construction companies involved in the fraud at Petrobras were many and hard to quantify".[^cite_note-citizen-163] Lula said that he and his party were being politically persecuted.[^cite_note-forbes-un-164][^cite_note-folha-lavajato-165][^cite_note-infomoney-lavajato-166]

On 16 March 2016, Rousseff appointed Lula as her [chief of staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_of_Staff_of_Brazil), a position comparable to that of prime minister. This would have shielded him from arrest due to the immunity that went with the position.[^cite_note-167] Cabinet ministers in Brazil are among close to seven hundred senior government officials enjoying special judicial standing, which means they can only be tried by Brazil's [Supreme Federal Court](3359076.md). Supreme Court Judge [Gilmar Mendes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gilmar_Mendes) suspended Lula's appointment on the grounds that Rousseff was trying to help Lula circumvent prosecution.[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169][^cite_note-bbcjudge-170][^cite_note-171]

On 28 July 2016, Lula filed a petition with the [United Nations Human Rights Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Human_Rights_Committee), a panel of experts, requesting that it provide a view on his accusation that Moro had violated his rights.[^cite_note-172] The Committee ultimately accepted the case. The Brazilian ambassador to the [UK](31717.md) wrote: "The (Brazilian) judiciary is fully independent and due process of law is faithfully observed. All defendants facing criminal prosecution fully enjoy the guarantee of a fair trial and the right to appeal."[^cite_note-173]

On 14 September 2016, prosecutors filed corruption charges against Lula, accusing him of being the mastermind or 'maximum commander of the scheme'.[^cite_note-174] On 19 September 2016, 13th Circuit ([Paraná](229411.md)) federal judge [Sergio Moro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergio_Moro), who was leading the corruption probe, accepted an indictment for money laundering against Lula and his wife [Marisa Letícia Lula da Silva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marisa_Let%C3%ADcia_Lula_da_Silva). On 11 May 2017, Lula answered a summons by appearing in [Curitiba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Curitiba) and was questioned by Moro. The closed-court hearing lasted five hours. Thousands of Lula supporters went to Curitiba, together with Dilma Rousseff. After the hearing, Lula and Rousseff gave speeches to his supporters; Lula attacked what he called bias in the Brazilian media.[^cite_note-175]

#### Guilty verdict and sentencing

Lula was found guilty by the lower court of accepting R\$3.7 million in bribes (\$940,000 US) in the form of improvements to his beachfront house, made by construction company [Grupo Metha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grupo_Metha?action=edit\&redlink=1), which in turn received lucrative contracts from the state-owned oil company [Petrobras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrobras).[^cite_note-nyt-prison-176] Lula also faced other charges, including money laundering, influence peddling and obstruction of justice.[^cite_note-177][^cite_note-nyt-prison-176] On 12 July 2017, [Sergio Moro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergio_Moro) sentenced Lula to nine and a half years in prison.[^cite_note-178] Lula remained free pending his appeal.[^cite_note-sentence-179] Lula's lawyer accused the judge of bias and the judge replied that nobody, not even the former president, should be above the [rule of law](25166191.md).[^cite_note-sentence-179]

On 25 January 2018, the Appeal Court of Porto Alegre found Lula guilty of corruption and money laundering and increased his sentence to 12 years of prison[^cite_note-180] for one of the nine charges, while the other eight were still pending. On 26 March 2018, that same court upheld its own sentence, thus ending the case in that court.[^cite_note-181]

#### Imprisonment

Main article: [Imprisonment of Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imprisonment_of_Luiz_In%C3%A1cio_Lula_da_Silva)

On 5 April 2018, Brazil's Supreme Federal Court (STF) voted 6–5 to deny Lula's *[habeas corpus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habeas_corpus)* petition.[^cite_note-182] The court ruled that Lula must begin serving his sentence relating to his 12 July 2017 graft conviction for taking bribes from an engineering firm in return for help to land contracts with state-run oil company [Petroleo Brasileiro SA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petroleo_Brasileiro_SA), despite him not having exhausted all of his appeals, but rather only one appeal, at which the appellate court had unanimously upheld his sentence.[^cite_note-183][^cite_note-184][^cite_note-auto1-185] Brazilian financial markets rallied, as the decision increased the chances a market-friendly candidate winning the election.[^cite_note-auto1-185] Lula and his party vowed to continue his campaign from prison following the court's decision that he must surrender himself by 6 April.[^cite_note-186] The head of Brazil's army, General Eduardo Villas Boas, called for Lula to be placed behind bars.[^cite_note-187] Following Judge Moro's issuance of an arrest warrant for Lula on 6 April 2018, Lula appealed to the UN Human Rights Committee to – in addition to his primary case – take emergency action by asking the government of Brazil to prevent his arrest until he had exhausted all appeals, arguing that the Brazilian Supreme Court had narrowly adopted its ruling with only six votes against five, which "shows the need for an independent court"; the Committee ultimately denied Lula's request.[^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189] In June 2018, the Committee denied Lula's request.[^cite_note-190]

Lula failed to turn himself in at the scheduled time,[^cite_note-191] but he did so on the following day on 7 April 2018.[^cite_note-192] After Lula was imprisoned, protesters took to the streets in cities across Brazil.[^cite_note-193] Lula's imprisonment led to the formation of the [Free Lula Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Lula_Movement).

On 28 May 2018, the UN Human Rights Committee initiated an investigation into Lula's case.[^cite_note-194] In August, the Committee "requested Brazil to take all necessary measures to ensure that Lula can enjoy and exercise his political rights while in prison, as candidate in the 2018 presidential elections"; Brazil's foreign ministry noted that the recommendation had no legal significance.[^cite_note-195][^cite_note-196]

On 8 July 2018, [on-call](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On-call) weekend duty federal judge for the 4th region Rogério Favreto ordered Lula's release.[^cite_note-197] Moro said that Favreto did not have the authority to release Lula, and Favreto's ruling was overturned the same day by the Judge Pedro Gebran Neto, president of the 4th regional court.[^cite_note-198]

On 2 August 2018, Pope Francis received three former allies of Lula in Rome: [Celso Amorim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celso_Amorim), [Alberto Fernández](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alberto_Fern%C3%A1ndez) and [Carlos Ominami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Ominami).[^cite_note-199] Later, the pope addressed a handwritten note to Lula (posted on his Twitter account) with the following text: "To Luiz Inacio Lula da Silva with my blessing, asking him to pray for me, Francisco".[^cite_note-200]

On 9 June 2019, *[The Intercept](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Intercept)* published [leaked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaza_Jato) [Telegram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telegram_\(app\)) messages between the judge in Lula's case, [Sergio Moro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sergio_Moro), and the Operation Car Wash lead prosecutor, [Deltan Dallagnol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deltan_Dallagnol), in which they allegedly conspired to convict Lula to prevent his candidacy for the 2018 presidential election.[^cite_note-201][^cite_note-202][^cite_note-203][^cite_note-204][^cite_note-205][^cite_note-206][^cite_note-207] Moro was accused of lacking impartiality in Lula's trial.[^cite_note-nytimes-208] Following the disclosures, the resumption of legal proceedings was determined by the Supreme Court.[^cite_note-209] Moro denied any wrongdoing or [judicial misconduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_misconduct) during the course of Operation Car Wash and his investigation of Lula, claiming that the conversations leaked by *The Intercept* were misrepresented by the press and that conversations between prosecutors and judges are normal.[^cite_note-210] Moro became [Minister of Justice and Public Security](40581099.md) after the election of president Jair Bolsonaro.

The information published by *The Intercept* prompted reactions both in Brazil and overseas. A group of 17 lawyers, ministers of Justice, and high court members from eight countries reacted to the leaks by describing former president Lula as a political prisoner and calling for his release.[^cite_note-choc-211] United States Senator [Bernie Sanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernie_Sanders) said Lula should be released and his conviction annulled. American Congressman [Ro Khanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ro_Khanna) asked the Trump administration to investigate Lula's case, saying that "Moro was a bad actor and part of a larger conspiracy to send Lula to jail".[^cite_note-212] A number of international intellectuals, activists and political leaders, including professor [Noam Chomsky](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noam_Chomsky) and 12 US Congressmen, complained that the legal proceedings appeared to be designed to prevent Lula from running for president in 2018.[^cite_note-213][^cite_note-214] American talk show host [Michael Brooks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Brooks_\(political_commentator\)), a vocal advocate for Lula, opined that Lula's imprisonment and Moro's alleged political motives had rendered the results of the 2018 election "fundamentally illegitimate".[^cite_note-215]

#### Release

On 8 November 2019, Lula was released from prison after 580 days when the Brazilian Supreme Court ended mandatory imprisonment of convicted criminals after their first appeal failed.[^cite_note-216][^cite_note-\:0-217][^cite_note-218][^cite_note-219][^cite_note-220][^cite_note-221] His release allowed him to remain out of prison until all of his appeals of his corruption and money laundering convictions were exhausted.[^cite_note-222]

On 27 November, the [Federal Regional Tribunal of Region 4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Regional_Tribunal_of_Region_4?action=edit\&redlink=1) in [Porto Alegre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porto_Alegre) increased Lula's sentence to 17 years.[^cite_note-223]

On 8 March 2021, Judge [Edson Fachin](54137879.md) of the Supreme Federal Court annulled all convictions against Lula, ruling that the court in Curitiba, in [Paraná](229411.md) state, which convicted him lacked jurisdiction to do so because the crimes he was accused of did not take place in that state, as at the time Lula resided in the capital, Brasilia.[^cite_note-auto4-224] Justice Fachin said the cases against him should therefore be retried by a court in that city.[^cite_note-225] The judge did not rule as to whether Lula was guilty or not of the corruption charges.[^cite_note-auto4-224] On 15 April, a full Supreme Court upheld the ruling in an 8–3 decision.[^cite_note-226]

On 23 March 2021, the Supreme Federal Court ruled by a 3–2 decision that Moro, who had overseen Lula's trial in a case, was biased against him.[^cite_note-biased-227] On 23 June it upheld the ruling in a 7–4 decision.[^cite_note-228] On 24 June, Judge Gilmar Mendes of the Supreme Federal Court annulled the two other cases Moro had brought against Lula, reasoning that there was a link between them and the case in which Moro was declared biased. This meant that all evidence Moro had collected against Lula was inadmissible in court, and that fresh trials would be needed.[^cite_note-Gilmar-229]

## Between terms (2011–2023)

### Health

On 29 October 2011, it was announced that Lula, a former smoker, had a [malignant tumor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malignant_tumor) in his [larynx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Larynx),[^cite_note-230] which was detected after his voice became unusually hoarse.[^cite_note-231] He had [chemotherapy](7172.md) to treat the tumor,[^cite_note-232] and was later treated with [radiation therapy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radiation_therapy); his [laryngeal cancer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laryngeal_cancer) went into remission. Lula announced his recovery in March 2012.[^cite_note-233]

On 21 January 2021, Lula tested positive for [COVID-19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Cuba) while participating in the filming of an [Oliver Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oliver_Stone) documentary in Cuba, five days after arriving on the island. He recovered without needing to be admitted to hospital.[^cite_note-234] On 13 March 2021, Lula received his first dose of the [CoronaVac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CoronaVac) vaccine.[^cite_note-235]

In October 2023, Lula had [hip joint replacement surgery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip_replacement) for a [hip prosthesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Implant_\(medicine\)), replacing the top of his right [femur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Femur) with an implant to treat his [arthrosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthrosis).[^cite_note-auto34-236][^cite_note-auto31-237] [^cite_note-238] He also had a [blepharoplasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blepharoplasty), a cosmetic plastic surgery to remove excess skin from both of his eyelids.[^cite_note-auto34-236][^cite_note-239][^cite_note-auto31-237]

### 2018 presidential campaign



Lula in 2016



Lula with Brazilian politicians [Manuela d'Ávila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuela_d'%C3%81vila) and [Marcelo Freixo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcelo_Freixo), 2018

In 2017, Lula announced he would stand as the Workers' Party candidate for president again in the [2018 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Brazilian_general_election). In September, he led a caravan of supporters which travelled through the states of Brazil, starting with Minas Gerais, whose governor was Lula's political ally [Fernando Pimentel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fernando_Pimentel).[^cite_note-240] While traveling through the [South of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Region,_Brazil), the caravan became the target of protests. In Paraná, a campaign bus was shot at, and in Rio Grande do Sul, rocks were thrown at pro-Lula militants.[^cite_note-241]

Despite Lula's imprisonment in April 2018, the Workers' Party kept Lula as the party's presidential candidate. In a poll conducted by [Ibope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian_Institute_of_Public_Opinion_and_Statistics) in June 2018, Lula led with 33% of vote intentions, with the [PSL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Liberal_Party_\(Brazil\)) candidate [Jair Bolsonaro](46603496.md) polling second with 15%.[^cite_note-242] Lula negotiated a national coalition with the [PCdoB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partido_Comunista_do_Brasil) and regional alliances with the [Socialist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partido_Socialista_Brasileiro).[^cite_note-243]

The Workers' Party officially nominated Lula as its candidate on 5 August 2018, in São Paulo. Actor [Sérgio Mamberti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A9rgio_Mamberti) read a letter written by Lula, who was unable to attend because of his prison sentence. Former São Paulo mayor Fernando Haddad was named as Lula's running mate and intended to represent Lula in events and debates. If Lula was declared ineligible, Haddad would replace Lula as candidate, with [Manuela d'Ávila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manuela_d'%C3%81vila) replacing Haddad as the vice-presidential candidate.[^cite_note-244]

In response to a petition from Lula, the [UN Human Rights Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Human_Rights_Committee) on 17 August 2018 suggested to the Brazilian government that it allow Lula to exercise his political rights.[^cite_note-245]

In a 26 August poll, Lula had 39 percent of vote intentions within one month of the first round. The same opinion polling put Lula ahead of all his challengers in a second round run-off, including the nearest one, [PSL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Liberal_Party_\(Brazil\)) candidate Jair Bolsonaro, by 52 to 32.[^cite_note-246]

Lula's [candidacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Candidate) was denied by the [Superior Electoral Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superior_Electoral_Court) on 31 August 2018 by a majority vote of the seven-judge panel.[^cite_note-247] On 11 September 2018, Lula officially withdrew and was replaced by Fernando Haddad, whom Lula endorsed.[^cite_note-248]

## Second presidency (2023–present)

Main article: [Second presidency of Lula da Silva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_presidency_of_Lula_da_Silva)

| Part of [a series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Progressivism) on |
| --- |
